● Yes, open it in the container, and choose "Live Documentation Dev (WSL2
  graphics card)". Do one paste first, so this chat comes with you.

  Ubuntu has no Node, on purpose: it is the disk, and the container is the
  workshop. Nothing of ours can be built or tested from this window.

  Before you click: one paste

  This chat's log lives in Ubuntu's home folder, and the container keeps its
  chats on a Docker volume. Reopening now would leave this chat behind. In this
  window's terminal:

  docker run --rm -u 1000:1000 -v live-documentation-claude:/to -v
  ~/.claude/projects/-home-user-src-Live-Documentation:/from:ro
  mcr.microsoft.com/devcontainers/typescript-node:22 cp
  /from/b4286088-8e81-4c34-abcf-7d5a8ca14c0d.jsonl
  /to/projects/-workspaces-Live-Documentation/

  It prints nothing when it works. I checked that it can read the log and write
  that folder. I could not test whether the extension then resumes a chat begun
  under another folder's path. If this chat is not in Claude Code's history
  inside the container, start a new one and say "continue 11/18"; today's record
  (AI-Agent-Workspace/ChatHistory/2026/11/2026-11-18.1.record.md) has
  everything.

  Then

  1. F1 → "Dev Containers: Reopen in Container" → Live Documentation Dev (WSL2
     graphics card). Use F1 if the pop-up does not ask which configuration.
  2. Wait for the set-up. Dependencies install into a fresh volume, as on your
     last rebuild. The log should end with "The host's graphics card is
     available to the browser tests." and "Devcontainer setup complete!".
  3. If the Claude Code panel is missing, install the extension into the
     container from the Extensions view. Your sign-in is already on the volume.
  4. Open this chat from its history and say you're in.

  1. Why the container

  Windows ── the VS Code window you see
     │
     └─ one WSL2 virtual machine: 20 threads, 62 GB, the RTX 3090
          ├─ Ubuntu: a disk. ~/src/Live-Documentation lives here.
          └─ Docker: the dev container. Node, the indexers, the browser
             tests and Claude Code. It mounts the folder above directly.

  - What I tried from here: a throwaway container with the two graphics-card
    arguments and this folder mounted. The folder shows as Linux's own disk, not
    the slow crossing to Windows, and the card's device and libraries are
    there.
  - What only your reopen can show: the dev container itself, and a browser run
    on the card inside it.
  - A first measure of the new disk: the markdown link audit took 104 s on D:\
    yesterday and 2.2 s here.

  2. Opening it normally

  - Docker Desktop running first, as before.
  - File → Open Recent → the entry for this folder marked [Dev Container: Live
    Documentation Dev (WSL2 graphics card) …]. I expect that one click to start
    Ubuntu and the container and attach; I have not seen the entry myself.
  - If it is not there: in an Ubuntu terminal, cd ~/src/Live-Documentation &&
    code ., then accept the reopen.
  - Retire the D:\ entry, and don't open that copy in a container again. Don't
    delete it yet either: it is the way back until the gate has passed here.
  - Unchanged: your preview (npx http-server dist/explorer -p 8999 -c-1 in a VS
    Code terminal) and pushing. Pushing is now the working copy's backup.

  Nothing else changes on your side. That configuration gives me everything, and
  I check the card at the start of each chat.

  3. Compute

  ┌───────────────────┬─────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
  │       What        │  WSL gives us   │        Of the machine's        │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Processor threads │ 20              │ 20 (10 cores)                  │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Memory            │ 62 GB           │ 128 GB (WSL's default is half) │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Graphics card     │ RTX 3090, 24 GB │ the same card                  │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Disk              │ 949 GB free     │ bounded by C:'s 745 GB free    │
  └───────────────────┴─────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

  - Nothing needs changing. There is no .wslconfig, so these are WSL's defaults.
    Ubuntu and Docker's containers share the one virtual machine, so a
    container gets all of it. The most memory I saw the container use was 9.4
    GB; the real limit on parallel tests is the ten cores.
  - Windows is not capping the processor: the power plan is Balanced with the
    maximum state at 100%.
  - To check yourself: nproc, free -g and /usr/lib/wsl/lib/nvidia-smi in any
    terminal here or in the container. The knob, if ever needed, is
    C:\Users\User\.wslconfig; I recommend not creating one.

❯ One more question from me to you:

  I can see that the management of the chat record takes up a lot of your
  _effort_. What isn't yet clear to me is whether that effort is fully worth
  it.

  Here are two alternatives I'm considering, and I'd ask which you think is
  better:
  - Continue with the chat record as-is: it is the most durable to changes
  (survived container rebuilds!!), and the time spent upkeeping it is time
  spent _honing one's ideas_. The root agent continues to be the owner of the
  chat record because it is worth the effort.
  - A scribe subagent-like setup: A model like Opus 5.5 Medium (and perhaps
  even smaller) is setup with hooks of some sort to activate when a completed
  response has been delivered to the user _or keeps itself active via keep-warm
  jobs like you do_. It cannot see into the mind of the root agent, but the
  root agent now gets to think about the task at hand and managing the builder
  fleet with the finite computational budget it has available. The scribe
  subagent simply tries to create an efficient markdown record which
  better-and-better approximates _what the user actually saw_ in the chat
  window (I notice that you mention failed reply deliveries a lot -- things
  like that could feasibly become something to no longer worry about).

  What do you think?

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● No rebuild is needed, and this chat is the stable one for the day. The
  container was created correctly; only its set-up script stopped at the build.
  I fixed the cause, ran the whole script again from inside the container, and
  it finished cleanly. This chat's log is on the chats volume, so it would
  survive a rebuild anyway.

  What broke, and the fix

  The fault was mine, in yesterday's move script.

  - Cause. TypeScript keeps a notebook of what it has already compiled
    (tsconfig.tsbuildinfo) so the next build only redoes what changed. The
    notebook sat beside each package's tsconfig.json; the compiled output sits
    in dist/. The move script carried the notebooks and left the dist/ folders
    behind.
  - Effect. The engine's build trusted its notebook and wrote 78 of its 565
    files. The Explorer compiles against the engine's output, found none of its
    types, and failed with the 54 errors you pasted.
  - Fix. One line in each of the four packages' tsconfig.json (for example
    packages/engine/tsconfig.json) puts the notebook inside dist/. A missing
    dist/ now always means a full build, including when someone deletes one by
    hand.
  - Checked. A build from nothing passes, and so does a build after deleting the
    engine's dist/ alone, which is the case that failed. The four Live Docs
    explain the line (for example the engine's
    (.mdmd/layer-4/packages/engine/tsconfig.json.mdmd.md)).

  Your two confirmations

  Both are recorded in direction. The session-history builder will be briefed on
  the convention as proposed. The playtest plan stands, with room for the
  variants you mentioned when we get there. Nothing from yesterday is waiting on
  you now.

  Who should keep the chat record

  I recommend neither option whole: split the record by kind of work. This
  turn's upkeep had two kinds.

  - Copying. Your messages and my delivered replies word for word, their times,
    the delivery check, file hashes. No judgment is involved, and I already do
    it with a script that reads the chat's log.
  - Authoring. The work notes, the resumption and Memory: what was found,
    decided and rejected, and why.

  What I'd do with each:

  1. Copying goes to a script the harness runs on a hook, at the end of each
     reply and at each new message, with no model involved. It is exact where a
     scribe would approximate, it cannot paraphrase you, and it admits only
     visible messages. It also has no life of its own, which answers your
     earlier worry about jobs that outlive their purpose.
  2. Authoring stays with the root, cut to what the delivered reply doesn't
     already say. A scribe cannot see why something was done, and by your rule
     it must not read reasoning, so it would either restate the transcript or
     guess at reasons. Your "honing" point held this turn: about to write down a
     reason for the fix's shape, I tested it first and it was false, so the
     record has the true one.
  3. One trial for a scribe model: the session summary at close, drafted from
     the committed record and reviewed by me. That is the largest single block
     of archive work, and its input is plain text that can be checked.

  A scribe would not fix missed deliveries either; it would only take over my
  bookkeeping about them, which the script does too. The log shows two of my six
  mid-work lines as messages this turn. What protects you is that everything
  you need is in the closing reply.

  If you agree, I'd build the hook as part of the session-history round, since
  that round moves where records live. I would first confirm that the VS Code
  extension fires hooks; I have not checked that yet.

  One reviewer (Opus, high effort, read-only), given the code and the
  specification. What it found, and what I did:
  - **Fixed before running:**
    - **High:** relink would have skipped all 90 probe, research and screenshot

  ### Forks I decided
  1. **`Antigravity/`** (Nov 2025, subfolders `11-19/` and `11-20/`) stays at
  month level, as received, because its name carries no full date. The
  alternative is `2025-11-19/Antigravity/<uuid>/...` and
  `2025-11-20/Antigravity/...` beside that day's transcript, which drops the
  received `11-19` level. I recommend leaving it.
  2. **Two harness exports named for the day after their session began** go by
  their name: `2026-10-08-035441-…txt` belongs to session 2026-10-07.1, and
  `2026-11-08-003022-…txt` to 2026-11-07.1. Both land in the next day's
  directory beside another session's record. The rule says this, and it matches
  how the four calendar-dated directories are treated. The alternative is a
  two-entry override justified by the month inventories, which is judgment per
  file. I recommend the rule as it is; the inventory links them correctly.
  3. **Links are read in markdown only** (`.md`, `.mdmd`). Link-shaped text in
  source comments counts as a path mention for milestone 2, because changing it
  means regenerating Live Docs.
  4. **The table** sits at `AgentSessionHistory/old-paths.md`, one row per
  renamed entry (312 rows), with paths in backticks rather than links. It is
  committed with the links commit, so the renames commit stays pure.
  5. **`Screenshots/README.md`** is left in place for milestone 2.

  ### Path mentions outside links: counts at the base, and a proposed rule
  **Counts.**
  - **Current documents** (guide, READMEs, Memory, Gallery, .mdmd including Live
  Docs' notes):
    - 63 name an existing path.
    - 34 name none: templates like `Probes/<date>/` and bare folders.
    - 2 sit inside generated regions; these come from source comments.
  - **Archive READMEs:** 1.
  - **Source and configuration outside the workspace:** 28 existing and 21
  templates or globs.
  - **Historical documents** (records, summaries, Notes):
    - 268 existing and 51 none, outside fences.
    - 378 and 71 inside fences.
  - **Probe, research and screenshot documents:** 117 existing and 8 none.
  - **Evidence scripts inside Probes and Screenshots:** 215. These are relative
  paths in `.ts`, `.mjs` and `.py` files; leave them as evidence.
  - **Captures:** about 4,370. Never altered.

  **Proposed rule:** re-point a mention mechanically only when all three hold:
  - it is in a current document or a source or config file, and outside a fence
  and a generated region;
  - it names an existing file or directory under the old folders;
  - it keeps its form: a path from the repository root stays from the root, a
  relative path stays relative.

  That changes about 92 mentions: 63 in documents, 28 in source, 1 in an archive
  README. Templates, globs and bare folder names go to milestone 2's authored
  edits, and historical documents keep their words.

  *"It's not a vulnerability … Don't let untrusted users define regular

  **npm's proposed 3.7.0 for the import resolver is a false fix.** It is a step
  back to the line that used fast-glob. fast-glob uses micromatch, which uses
  picomatch ^2.3.1, and that is still inside the advisory's "≤4.0.7" range. npm
  suggests it only because it marks tinyglobby as vulnerable in every version.

  ### Fork: picomatch, tinyglobby, fdir and the import resolver
  - **Who pulls picomatch:** tinyglobby (through typescript-estree and the
  import resolver), vite and vitest. Every one of those is development tooling;
  the lock marks picomatch dev-only. The engine, Explorer and CLI never load it.
  - **What patterns it sees:** only ones we wrote ourselves, in
  eslint.config.js, tsconfig project globs and vitest.config.ts. It never sees
  untrusted input in our use.
  - **Options:**
    - (a) Wait for npm's audit data to sync the withdrawal. The 14 should then
  vanish with no change from us.
    - (b) An override has no patched picomatch to point at.
    - (c) A different resolver would not help, because vite, vitest and
  typescript-estree pull picomatch too.
    - (d) Ignoring the advisory would break the "causes, not gates" rule.
  - **Recommendation: (a).** I'll re-run `npm audit` in milestone 2 and tell you
  whether npm has caught up.

  ### glob and minimatch: what changed for us
  **Where we call them:**
  - minimatch: `packages/engine/src/live-docs/relatedDocs.ts:149`
  (`{dot:true}`).
  - glob, with `ignore` lists:
    - `packages/engine/src/live-docs/discovery.ts:263` (also withFileTypes, dot,
  windowsPathsNoEscape)
    - `packages/engine/src/live-docs/adapters/java.ts:533`, `csharp.ts:923`,
  `rust.ts:584`, `go.ts:458`
    - `packages/generator/src/generator.ts:332`
    - `scripts/slopcop/check-asset-paths.ts:159`, `check-markdown-links.ts:154`,
  `check-symbols.ts:191`
  - glob, without `ignore`:
    - `packages/engine/src/live-docs/graphFiles.ts:28`
    - `packages/generator/src/generator.ts:395`
    - `scripts/doc-tools/documentationLinks.ts:661` (dot:false)
    - `scripts/live-docs/lint.ts:146`
    - `tests/integration/live-docs/round-trip.test.ts:34`,
  `powershell-oracle.test.ts:46`
  - Our own `matchesPathPattern` (archetype.ts) does not use either library.

  **What changed, from the changelogs and type declarations:**
  - **How patterns are matched.** minimatch 11 matches each path segment with a
  new non-backtracking matcher (`@isaacs/nonrecursive-glob-match`, a 3-week-old
  package by the same author) instead of a RegExp.
  - **Removed APIs.** `makeRe`, `.regexp`, `AST`, `debug` and
  `maxExtglobRecursion` are gone. We use none of them.
  - **Trailing slashes.** A path ending in `/` no longer matches an extglob at
  the end of a pattern. None of our patterns end in an extglob, but this matters
  because glob's ignore code tests paths with a trailing slash.
  - **glob 14's `ignore`.** Patterns starting with `/` are now rooted, and
  ignore patterns follow `windowsPathsNoEscape`. We have no leading-slash or
  backslash ignore patterns.
  - **Unchanged for us:** the option names we use, the named exports
  `glob`/`globSync`/`minimatch`, the CommonJS and ESM entry points, and the
  withFileTypes `Path` methods (path-scurry 3 changes only an internal getter).
  - **Node:** minimatch 11 and lru-cache need 20 or ≥22; .nvmrc and engines say
  22. Fine.

  **Early evidence that both libraries select exactly the same files:**
  - I ran minimatch 10.2.6 and 11.0.9 side by side from unpacked tarballs, pure
  string matching, about a second of CPU.
  - Inputs: every tracked path and directory in the worktree plus 22 edge cases.
  Patterns: every one in .live-docs.config.json, slopcop.config.json, the three
  DEFAULT_IGNORE and DEFAULT_PATTERNS lists, the engine and adapter walks, the
  tests and eslint.config.js.
  - Each was run with its call site's options, and again under glob's ignore
  settings (level 2, nonegate, nocomment, with and without the trailing slash).
  - **Result: 665,411 plus 897,904 comparisons, 0 differences.** Script:
  `/tmp/claude-1000/-workspaces-Live-Documentation/6450141c-9e11-40e5-95a5-16612
  1c231a3/scratchpad/minimatch-compare.mjs`

  ### Something you should know: npm 10.9.9 cannot resolve this lock
  - npm 10.9.9 (also 10.9.11) crashes with `Cannot read properties of null
  (reading 'edgesOut')`. It happens as soon as vite moves past 8.3.1: vite
  8.3.8's optional `@vitejs/devtools ^0.8` peer chain reaches `vitest@*`
  (5.1.1), and npm's peer-set code fails.
  - So I resolved the lock with `npx npm@11.21.3 install --package-lock-only`. I
  then ran the container's npm 10 over the result, which accepted it unchanged
  apart from removing npm 11's `libc` fields. The committed lock is therefore in
  npm 10's format.
  - Anyone who later re-resolves vite with npm 10 will hit the same crash.
  Upgrading the container's npm is a separate decision for you.

  ### The lock's net version changes
  - glob 14.0.6, minimatch 11.0.9 (+ @isaacs/nonrecursive-glob-match 1.1.0),
  path-scurry 3.0.0, lru-cache 11.5.3.
  - eslint 10.15.0, @eslint/config-array 0.23.6, typescript-eslint 8.73.0,
  eslint-plugin-import-x 4.18.1.
  - vitest family 4.1.12, vite 8.3.8, rolldown 1.2.19.
  - eslint-import-resolver-node and its four dependencies are removed: import-x
  4.18 marks that peer optional, and our config uses the TypeScript resolver.
  - An optional wasm32 rolldown binding is added; it does not install on this
  platform.
  - I did not take vitest 5. The V4 line, 4.1.12, is the patched one.

● No reason not to, so I'm raising it to npm 11 as part of the dependency round.
  There is one caution about how, and it is covered.

  - The caution: two npm versions that both write the lockfile undo each other's
    work; npm 10 strips fields that npm 11 writes. Only the container writes
    the lock. The four GitHub workflows run npm ci, which only reads it, so they
    can stay on Node 22's own npm 10 for now.
  - What still has to be proven: that npm 10's npm ci installs from a lock
    written by npm 11. I've added that to the dependency builder's next
    milestone.
  - How: one pinned line in .devcontainer/post-create.sh, which both container
    configurations run. It takes effect at your next rebuild, and I'll run the
    same command here so this container needs no rebuild today.
  - Related, already granted: on November 15 you said of moving Node from 22 to
    26 "If it's straightforward, go ahead". A newer Node ships npm 11, which
    would put the container and the workflows back on one npm. That is a bigger
    change with its own gate, so I'm keeping it out of today's round.

  One consequence on GitHub: npm-audit.yml runs every Monday at 04:00 UTC and
  fails on any high finding. Until npm's data picks up the withdrawal of the
  picomatch advisory, it will go red on those 14 findings. That is npm's stale
  data, not something this round can close.

  known race. The other five passed at one worker this morning:

  - history-scrubber.spec.ts:180, :391 and :569
  - layout-lab.spec.ts:20 (repository chain)
  - local-map-branches.spec.ts:276

  Four of those five are the cases that sat on their time limits yesterday. So
  two workers is on course to be much faster, but it does not yet pass what one

  ### 2. The design

  **Reading** (`tests/e2e/scenarios/reading.ts`). It holds:
  - the scenario, the map, the settled address and the viewport;
  - the frame (`#view-map .map-edges`) and the picture (`.local-layout`);
  - the edge signs (`data-above`, `data-below`, `data-left`, `data-right`), the
  scale and the look (`data-look`);
  - the cards: id, box, whether subject, whether pinned, and the directory band
  they stand in;
  - the dots (`.symbol-anchor.dot`, inbound or outbound);
  - the wires: key, consumer, provider, kind (route, stub, run, back), sampled
  points, which end is the provider's, cards crossed, samples in a stranger's
  lane, opacity and filter;
  - the directories (`data-segments` on `.local-directory-band`, with parent and
  whether drawn) and the lanes (`.local-pass-through`, box and slots);
  - text faults, and the count of text boxes read;
  - the tone, reduced to contrast ratios;
  - console errors, off-site requests, and the screenshot's name.

  **Instrument** (`instrument.ts`). It reuses `readPicture` for the wires and
  card boxes, the design audit for collisions and cut text, and `contrast.ts`.
  One extra evaluate reads only what those do not. It reads the wires in
  `readPicture`'s order and refuses to report if the two lists disagree.
  `watchPage` collects console errors and off-site requests. The screenshot is
  saved beside the JSON under `reports/scenarios/<language>/`.

  **Judgments** (`judgments.ts`). Each is a pure function returning kept, broken
  or not applicable, with every finding listed.

  - **Floor:** the subject is drawn, named files are drawn or pinned, at least N
  cards and N wires, lanes reserved, a picture drawn, and some text read.
  - **Laws (sixteen):**
    1. no error and no request to another site;
    2. no text collides or is cut;
    3. every card stands inside its directory;
    4. each directory is one connected shape;
    5. sibling directories never overlap;
    6. uses enter on the left and offerings leave on the right (wire ends and
  dots);
    7. every route reads forward;
    8. no route crosses a card it does not end at;
    9. no lane lies in a stranger's directory;
    10. no route rises above the picture;
    11. a picture with no subject is framed whole, by the map's own padding
  rule;
    12. the edge signs are honest;
    13. the subject stands inside the frame;
    14. no wire glows, and every route is drawn at one strength;
    15. a card's names lead its prose, each legible;
    16. a directory's outline is ground, not figure.
  - **Relation:** a reload gives the same picture.
  - **Oracle:** the subject's wires, file to file, equal the compiler's edges at
  that file (`expected/compiler-edges.json`, read with the existing
  `readOracleEdges`). Ruby has no compiler edges, so it is reported not
  applicable.
  - **Baselines:** none.

  `judgments.test.ts` builds a hand-made good picture that keeps every law. Each
  judgment then gets a picture that breaks it, and the test checks the exact
  findings.

  **Token resolver** (`tokens.ts`, with `tokens.test.ts`).
  - The token table is the Rosetta manifest's part patterns, matched
  case-insensitively against the map's own file ids.
  - `{part}` is the part's subject file. It prefers a file named for the part,
  then an implementation over its header, then the declared entry for `main`.
  - `{part/}` is the part's own directory, only where nothing of another part
  shares it: Java, C# and Go.
  - `{part*}` is every file of the part.
  - An optional root prefix lets the same table resolve on the repository's own
  map, which holds the TypeScript Rosetta.
  - A token it cannot resolve raises an error, and the spec reports the scenario
  skipped as not applicable rather than passed.
  - Over the real folders, every source file of every program is placed except
  Python's `src/core_types.py`. That is fork 1.

  **Scenario table** (`table.ts`), with frame size in brackets:

  | Scenario | Frame | Floor | Also judged by |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | `{processor}` in focus | 1600×1000 | the subject, main and helpers drawn, at
  least 2 wires | the reload relation and the compiler oracle |
  | The whole program retained (`{main}`, `{processor}`, `{models*}`,
  `{types*}`, `{helpers}`) | 1600×1000 | all five pinned, at least 5 wires,
  lanes reserved | |
  | `{models/}` opened with nothing chosen | 1440×900 | – | Java, C# and Go only
  |
  | The root with nothing chosen | 1280×800 | – | |

  `still-pictures.spec.ts` runs the four scenarios over the nine samples at
  `/samples/rosetta/<language>/`. Every verdict is a soft expectation, and every
  verdict is also written as an annotation and to JSON.

  **Rosetta samples.**
  - `scripts/live-docs/visualize-rosetta.ts` reads the manifest's variants and
  builds each one into `dist/explorer/samples/rosetta/<language>/`.
  - The build body of `visualize-sample.ts` moved into a new shared
  `sample-build.ts`, so the estate and Rosetta builds use one function. That is
  a small edit to an existing script.
  - There is a new npm script, `live-docs:visualize:rosetta`, and `test:e2e` now
  runs it before Playwright.

  ### 3. Which spec files the table covers, and why

  The ledger has each case's details. Together these are 15 runs taking 318 s.

  ### 4. Forks for you

  1. **Token table gap.** The manifest's types patterns miss Python's
  `core_types.py`. Subjects of parts with several files are inferred: Java
  models picks `ModelFactory.java`, C's processor picks `processor.c`.
     - (a) Add `core_types.*` to the manifest and keep the inference.
     - (b) An explicit part-to-files map per variant in the manifest.
     - (c) Leave it, and Python scenarios that need `{types}` stay not
  applicable.
     - I recommend (a): one word in a fixture I don't own. The resolver test
  currently asserts today's truth, Python unplaced.
  2. **The oracle.** File-level compiler edges will likely go red where an
  adapter's meaning differs from the compiler's. C's compiler records
  `processor.c` to `helpers.c`, definition to definition, where the docs record
  includes of the header.
     - (a) Two-sided against the compiler, red until the adapters match.
     - (b) The compiler, with the gaps `oracle:compare` already reports kept as
  a recorded, re-approved baseline per language, one-sided.
     - (c) Also judge the drawn wires against the bundle's own graph, which
  checks the drawing against its data (not independent).
     - I recommend (c) plus (b), decided once milestone 2 shows the real gaps.
  3. **Zoom is not in the address.** Scenarios at a look need Zoom Out presses.
     - (a) Allow a short list of presses before reading, in the scenario.
     - (b) Carry the look in the address, which is a product change.
     - I recommend (a). The first table needs neither.
  4. **The membrane's inset is not published.** The law holds a card inside its
  segment, weaker than today's 12 px literal.
     - (a) Publish `data-inset` beside `data-segments` in `branch-renderer.ts`,
  a one-line product change.
     - (b) Accept the weaker law.
     - I recommend (a).
  5. **Wire kind and style** are read by a second pass aligned with
  `readPicture`.
     - (a) Keep it that way.
     - (b) Add three fields to `readPicture`'s wire reading, an additive change
  to today's instrument file.
     - I recommend (b), if touching `still-picture.ts` is acceptable.
  6. **Our own map's seat.** The same table can run on `/` against the
  TypeScript Rosetta inside the repository, through the root prefix. That costs
  the repository's slower load. I recommend adding it in milestone 2 as a tenth
  map.
  7. **Where the ledger lives.** It is a dated measurement. I recommend you keep
  it in the session's Probes rather than in `tests/`.
  8. **The new spec joins the full suite.** Playwright's file match picks it up,
  and `test:e2e` now builds nine more samples. I recommend that, since the
  table is meant to replace cases. The alternative is its own npm script.

  ### Pinned npm: 11.21.1
  - **Why this version:** it was released 2026-10-15T15:45Z, about five weeks
  ago. The two later releases are too young: 11.21.2 came out 2026-11-04T22:39Z,
  which was 13 days 18 hours old when I chose, and 11.21.3 came out today.
  - **Supports our Node:** its engines field is `^20.17.0 || >=22.9.0`.
  - **It resolves the tree:** I re-resolved the lock from scratch with `npx -y
  npm@11.21.1 install --package-lock-only` starting from `0be65840`'s lock. It
  produced the same versions as milestone 1's lock. The only difference is where
  one copy of es-module-lexer sits in the tree.
  - **`.devcontainer/post-create.sh`:** a new step, placed before `npm install`,
  runs `npm install -g npm@11.21.1` and prints the version. The comment beside
  it says that npm 10.9 cannot resolve the tree once vite is past 8.3.1 and
  quotes the error it dies with.

  Base Environment" saying the container carries npm 11 and why, and that npm 10
  still installs from the lock with `npm ci`. All headings are unchanged.
  - **Not added:** no packageManager or engines.npm field. I did not touch the
  container's global npm.

  ### npm 11 now warns about install scripts
  npm 11 prints a warning that esbuild, tree-sitter-cli and unrs-resolver "have
  install scripts not yet covered by allowScripts".
  - **In npm 11 this is only a warning; the scripts still run.** I confirmed it:
  the `tree-sitter` binary that tree-sitter-cli's install script downloads is
  present.
  - **npm 12 blocks those scripts by default** (its 12.0.0 changelog says so).
  If the container ever moves to 12, the project will need an `allowScripts`
  field in package.json. That is a decision for later; I added nothing for it.

  ### The two npm proofs
  The lock's sha256 was `e92d60ef…cdd00d3` throughout, and after every step
  `cmp` found it unchanged byte for byte.
  - **(i) npm 10.9.9, as the workflows use it:** `npm --version` printed 10.9.9;
  `npm ci` exited 0 ("found 0 vulnerabilities"). The lock was unchanged.
  - **(ii) the pinned npm 11:** `npx -y npm@11.21.1 ci` exited 0, then `npx -y
  npm@11.21.1 install` exited 0. The lock was unchanged after each.
  - **Installed tree:** `npm ls --all` exits 0 under npm 10 and under npm
  11.21.1.

  ### What the newer tools flagged, and the fix
  - **Only `import-x/order`, 274 errors in 153 files.** eslint,
  typescript-eslint and the rest of import-x reported nothing new.
  - **Cause:** import-x 4.18.0 changed how its order rule alphabetizes (#517).
  Within one group, a parent import (`../x`) now always sorts before a sibling
  (`./y`), as eslint-plugin-import does. Our files followed the old order, which
  put siblings first.
  - **Fix:** I applied eslint's own fixer for that rule, nothing else.
    - Every changed file only moves import lines. In each of the 153 files the
  lines added equal the lines removed, no blank line changed, and no line other
  than an import changed.
    - Files by folder: packages/engine 16, packages/explorer 57,
  scripts/layout-lab 17, scripts/live-docs 4, tests/e2e 58, tests/integration 1.
  `git show --stat 310a2f7b` lists them all.
  - These files under packages/ changed only because the upgrade required it.

  ## Interim: your six decisions are done in code, nothing has been run, and I
  am waiting for the gate to end

  Per your instruction there were no builds, tests, browser or lint. Three new
  commits sit on top of c1523a52 in my worktree:

  1. **2f962956, the design audit's blind spot** (its own commit).
     - `truncations()` in `tests/e2e/design-audit.ts` now reads, for each piece
  of text, the clips of every element between the audited element and the text,
  from the text outward, before the audited element's own clip and its
  ancestors'.
     - A clip that scrolls still only narrows what is visible, so it reports
  nothing.
     - The bad case is a new browser fixture in `design-audit.spec.ts`. A name
  clipped to 40 px by its own span must be reported, more than 100 px hidden.
  Beside it, the same span set to scroll must not be.
     - It is not run yet. In your window I will run every spec that uses the
  audit and list each case that turns red, without quieting any.
  2. **215819ee, the token rule, the floors and the new judgments.**
     - **The token rule.** A part's subject is the file of the part that the
  most files of other parts use, read from the map's own references
  (`graph.links`). A tie goes to the file named for the part, then to the
  implementation. Without references, the C family's header goes before its
  implementation.
       - Unit test: in C, the processor resolves to `processor.h`. Its own
  `processor.c` counts for nothing, and a tie between `helpers.h` and
  `helpers.c` goes to `helpers.c`.
       - Unit test: in Java, models resolves to the most-used `Record.java`.
       - The test over the real folders, with no references, gives C
  `processor.h`.
     - **Floors.** Focus scenarios ask only for the subject, at least 2 cards
  and at least 1 wire. The retained scenario keeps every file of each part
  pinned (`{main*}` … `{helpers*}`), at least 2 cards, at least 1 wire, and
  lanes. The directory scenario asks for `{models*}` drawn.
     - **The four uncarried promises.** Each has a bad reading it must reject:
       - three laws: every directory but the root draws an outline (and the root
  none); a picture with no subject stands its root's columns at one top; a
  description and the Purpose are one tone;
       - one relation: held names keep their tones three presses out. The far
  scenario now reads the picture at reading size before its presses, in the same
  run, and judges the pair.
     - **The law that no name breaks inside a word** is written with its unit
  test. "Process / orConfig" is rejected; "Processor / Config" and "rosetta- /
  manifest.json" are kept. It sits in `PENDING_LAWS`, outside the table, and the
  test asserts that it stays out. It is ready for when the defect is mended.
       - Its seam rule is written independently of the product's
  `word-seams.ts`, so the judgment does not grade the map by the map's own rule.
       - The instrument now reads the lines each name is drawn on, letter by
  letter, so the law needs no further work to join the table.
     - **The corrected laws' comments** now say what the screenshots showed:
       - each dot stands at the end of its row, about 21 px inside the card's
  edge at scale 1;
       - farther out, "Intern…" and "Inter…" end in an ellipsis by design.

  ## The design
  - **The ring rule is a pure function now:** `ringedSpheres(beyond,
  groupWrappers)` in forceGraphWrappers.ts. With Off, every sphere with a count
  above zero wears a ring, exactly as today (the old `!isWrapped` test was
  always true under Off). With skin, cloud or cloud-rim, nothing does. In
  forceGraphView.ts, `hasRing` reads a `ringed` set computed once per render,
  and the key's `rings:` flag is `ringed.size > 0`, so the "More connected" row
  disappears whenever any wrapper is on.
  - **A lone hidden file is drawn as itself.**
    - In forceGraphShells.ts the partition's `loose` field is renamed `lone`.
  `shellPicture` now treats a lone file as its own wire end, the same way it
  treats a released file. Its wire to a shown file becomes the picture's
  ordinary wire, and its references to files inside a shell are bundled into a
  wire to that shell.
    - `beyond` now counts lone files too, so with wrappers on the only thing it
  counts is hidden documents. That count still feeds the name's tooltip ("N more
  connected, not shown").
    - The view places each lone file one hop past the hops shown, as it does
  released files. `atFrontier` already dims it, because it isn't in `shells`,
  `released`, `cells` or `loneNames`.
    - A press on it goes the ordinary file route (`onToggleNode`), which pins
  it.
  - **Hidden documents:** no ring and no mark of any kind. The tooltip count
  stays.
  - **Group Wrappers Off:** unchanged.
  - **Documents and notes:** the design document's passages (Directory shells,
  the Structure open items, the key paragraph, Discovery) and the authored notes
  of five Live Docs now match the mend. Each cites the 2026-11-16 record's Turn
  12 with a relative link. Regenerating the Live Docs waits for milestone 2.

  ## Forks
  1. **What a press on a lone file does.** "What a press on a file held in a
  shell does" is ambiguous in the code: you can't press a file inside a shell,
  because the press lands on the shell and pops it.
     - **Built:** a press pins the file. That is what the Structure layout's
  lone file does, what a released file does, and what a press on any dimmed
  frontier sphere does. So dimming keeps one meaning: press to ask about it.
     - **Alternative:** the first press "pops" it (makes it bright, nothing
  more) and the second pins it. That gives dimming two meanings, so I recommend
  the built version.
  2. **Dimmed in Connections, bright in Structure.**
     - **Built:** dimmed, as the owner was told.
     - **Alternatives:** dim Structure's lone file too, or draw the Connections
  one bright.
     - **Recommendation:** keep it as built, and show the owner the two pictures
  side by side in milestone 2.
  3. **The Structure frontier hub with some files hidden** now has no sign
  except the frontier's dimming.
     - **Alternatives:** keep a ring there only (two signs again), or bubble its
  hidden remainder (consistent, not built).
     - **Recommendation:** accept no sign for now.
  4. **The tooltip's "N more connected, not shown"** is kept for hidden
  documents. It is hover text, not a mark. Alternative: drop it while wrappers
  are on. I recommend keeping it.

● You have the product right. My word "hidden" was standing for two different
  things, and the ring is only about one of them. A ring never counts a file
  kind you have switched off.

  Two reasons a thing is not drawn

  - Switched off. The four switches (Show Tests, Show Assets, Show Related Docs,
    Show Citing Docs) are applied first, and the map is built from what is
    left. Hops, islands, membranes and every "more" count are worked out
    afterwards (forceGraphView.ts:1323-1343
    (packages/explorer/src/client/views/forceGraphView.ts#L1323-L1343),
    forceGraphScope.ts:143-148
    (packages/explorer/src/client/views/forceGraphScope.ts#L143-L148)). So a
    switched-off kind is never counted or hinted at. The one exception is a file
    you pinned or selected yourself, which is drawn whatever its kind.
  - Beyond the hops. The kind is on, but the thing is farther from what you
    asked about than the hops shown. Only this case has ever had a sign.

  My bullet "Hidden documents: no sign at all" meant the second: documents you
  have switched on that stand one hop past the edge of the picture.

  Your screenshot, concretely

  You had documents on and archetype.ts pinned at one hop.

  - The documents of archetype.ts itself are drawn.
  - A neighbouring file at the edge is drawn too, but that neighbour's documents
    are two hops from your pin, so they are not.
  - Code files in that position are gathered into a membrane. Documents never
    are, so each such neighbour kept a ring. That was nine of your ten rings.

  What you hope for is how it works today

  I measured this on November 17 from the built graph:

  ┌──────────────────────────────┬───────┐
  │ archetype.ts pinned, one hop │ Rings │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼───────┤
  │ Documents on                 │ 10    │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼───────┤
  │ Documents off                │ 0     │
  └──────────────────────────────┴───────┘

  The structure changes with the switches too, not only the counts. From the
  code: with a kind off, files that were joined only through it fall apart into
  separate islands, and a membrane holds only files of kinds that are on. The 2D
  map applies the two file switches the same way before it lays anything out
  (controller.ts:1212-1223
  (packages/explorer/src/client/views/localView/controller.ts#L1212-L1223)).

  I've written your words into direction as a rule to keep ("only reports the
  connections that are of the activated file archetypes"), so it is a promise
  and not an accident of the code.

  The mend is unchanged, in better words

  With wrappers on, a thing beyond the hops is shown as:

  - Files of one directory: a membrane, as now.
  - A lone file: itself, dimmed (new).
  - Documents: nothing of their own (new). The edge of the picture is already
    dimmed, which says a press shows more.

  The last one is the real choice, and yours to overrule when you see the
  pictures. The alternatives are keeping the ring for that one case, or
  membranes of documents.

  I'm adding one more pair to the builder's pictures: your state with documents
  on and with documents off, so you can see the structure change with the
  switch. The design document will say "switched off" and "beyond the hops",
  never "hidden" for both.

❯ >Beyond the hops. The kind is on, but the thing is farther from what you
  asked about than the hops shown. Only this case has ever had a sign.

  Ahhhhhhh okay. Great point: if it is no more visually efficient to create a
  membrane for one further hop away, might as well just draw that terminal leaf
  node, eh? Flipside of that coin: this could also be telling us that we
  should be more serious about _completely hiding_ things which are more than a
  configured set of hops away, and ensuring that the user has more ability to
  "scrub through" the emergent topology of the scanned directory without
  needing to see the remaining totality, even if encapsulated, all at once. I
  love being able to see everything, but I also recognize that not being able
  to see everything is plausibly quite important, and it would be interesting
  to me if we saw membranes kind of form and break and re-form as new hops were
  taken. I am trying to keep my mind open to the possibility that this design
  nit is a signal of something wider or slightly different than just ensuring
  the nit is resolved.

● I think the nit does point at something wider, and the two sides of your coin
  are one rule. I've started a trial so you can judge it by eye; it changes no
  default.

  What a pinned picture holds today

  With archetype.ts pinned at one hop and Skin on, the Connections picture holds
  three things:

  1. The hops you asked for.
  2. Every other file of the same connected group, however far away, gathered
     into membranes by directory, with wires between the membranes.
  3. The seed of every group the hops cannot reach, dimmed.

  So the picture we call Local is really the whole scan, most of it
  encapsulated. A membrane today means "everything else in this directory", not
  "what is one step from here".

  Your thought as one rule

  Call it the horizon: what is exactly one step past the last hop gets a sign;
  what is farther gets nothing until a step brings it to the edge.

  - Two or more files of a directory, one step away: a membrane.
  - One file alone: the file itself, your "terminal leaf". It is the rule's
    smallest case, not a special case.
  - Take a hop, pin a file at the edge, or pop a membrane: the membranes at the
    old edge break into files and new ones form at the new edge. That is your
    "form and break and re-form".

  Designers have names for the two stances:

  - Shneiderman's "overview first, zoom and filter, then details on demand"
    always shows everything. On our screen that is the archipelago with nothing
    pinned.
  - Van Ham and Perer's "search, show context, expand on demand" starts from
    what you asked about and grows a step at a time. It rests on Furnas's
    "degree of interest": a thing's claim to be drawn falls with its distance
    from your focus. On our screen that is what a pin would give under the
    horizon.

  Today's pinned picture tries to be both at once.

  What you said before that bears on it

  - November 2: you found it "so hard to find the islands/orphans", which is why
    the seeds of unreached groups stand beside every pinned picture. The same
    day you proposed an "isolate" mode ("I can really see the use case for
    both"), which became Local/Whole.
  - November 7: this repository pinned at two hops was "still very noisy. I
    backed that down to 1 hop and found myself having an easier time getting
    around". The far membranes are part of that noise, since they say nothing
    about your next step.
  - November 7: you loved the embryo look most on the archipelago with nothing
    pinned. The horizon leaves that picture alone.

  Nothing like the horizon has been tried or refused before.

  The trade

  - Lost: the standing overview inside a pinned picture. It stays one step away:
    the archipelago when nothing is pinned, the Whole toggle, and the World
    Map.
  - Gained: a membrane that means one thing, and a pinned picture whose size
    follows your question, not the repository's. There is also less to build
    before a pinned picture can show, because today every far membrane is packed
    first.
  - Risk: fewer embryos in a pinned picture, and you may miss them. That is for
    your eye.

  What I've started

  A trial lever in the 3D Tuning beside Hops: today's picture stays the default,
  and the horizon is the other value. It is Connections layout only. The rings
  builder is writing it (same module), in separate commits so the ring mend can
  land without it.

  You'll get:

  - The same pinned state both ways at one, two and three hops, from one camera.
  - At each, the counts of files drawn, files in membranes, and files not in the
    picture, plus the build time.
  - If practical, a clip of Hops dragged from 1 to 3 under the horizon.

  Three things I left out of the trial on purpose:

  - Documents one step away. They keep no sign, as in the mend. They could come
    under the horizon later.
  - The Structure layout. Its bubbles hold a directory's whole subtree, which is
    the same question in another form.
  - Where the Hops control lives. If scrubbing turns out to be the point, it
    deserves a place on the toolbar rather than inside Tuning.

  The scenario builder still has the machine (37 minutes so far) and has not
  handed back. Your turn and your sixth save of the export are recorded
  (45069ead).

  missing on each of the C and C++ programs, because our adapters record the
  include of a header where the compiler records the use of a definition. That
  is yours to rank.

› Message from general-purpose
  **The "Beyond the Hops" trial is written and committed as dd3c2cbf, on top of
  the ring mend (87f14e2c and 16150236), so the mend can land without it.
  Nothing has been built or run yet; the browser is still the other builder's.**

  ## What it is, for a reader not in the code
  Pin a file in the 3D map with Skin on, and today you see three things:
  - the file and its hops;
  - every other file of the same connected group, however far away, gathered
  into membranes by folder;
  - one seed for every other group the hops don't reach.

  The new choice sits in Tuning → 3D Layout → Discovery, under Group Wrappers.
  - **"Everything"** is today's picture and stays the default.
  - **"Next step"** draws only what is exactly one step past what stands in the
  picture:
    - two or more such files of one folder become a membrane;
    - a single one is drawn as itself;
    - nothing farther appears until a step brings it to the edge;
    - popping a membrane releases its files, and the files one step past them
  form the next membrane;
    - the seeds of unreached groups are not drawn.

  What does not change under "Next step":
  - The start with nothing pinned (the archipelago the owner loves) is the same
  under both.
  - So are the Structure layout and Group Wrappers Off.

  **Names I propose:** the lever "Beyond the Hops", with "Everything" and "Next
  step", as you suggested. "Next step" matches the map's own words "hop" and
  "step". Its tooltip: "With wrappers on, what stands past the hops: every file
  of the groups shown, or only what is one step away."

  Your reading of today's picture is right. Two nuances:
  - The shells only see groups shown in part. A group seen by its seed alone is
  wrapped whole.
  - Today the unreached seeds were also passed to the shells as "shown". That
  made no difference, since they belong to other groups.

  ## What I built
  - **The rule:** `nextStepFiles` in forceGraphShells.ts, used by `shellPicture`
  through a new optional `reach` field. It starts from the shown files and
  takes their neighbours. Whatever a pop releases then stands, and its
  neighbours join, until nothing new arrives. Documents are never held.
  - **The seeds:** `withoutUnreachedSeeds` in forceGraphScope.ts. It drops the
  unreached seeds and keeps a seed that was asked about or reached.
  - **The view** applies both only with something asked about, wrappers on, the
  Connections layout and "Next step".
  - **The setting:** a `beyondHops` type, default and markup, saved and carried
  in a link only at "Next step". Following the 2D trials, a link that carries 3D
  settings without it opens at "Everything".
  - **The control-count tests** go from 67 to 68, in both tuning-levers.test.ts
  and tests/e2e/tuning.spec.ts. That spec isn't in my named files; it was a
  one-number change, and the gate would otherwise go red.
  - **The design document** has a new paragraph after Directory shells, written
  as a trial under way. It quotes the owner's words of Turn 10 of the November
  18 record and their November 7 "still very noisy" (Turn 8 of the November 6
  record). Short notes were added to five Live Docs.

  **Unit cases:**
  - In the shells tests:
    - a ring forms beside what is shown, and nothing two steps away is held;
    - a pop breaks a membrane and forms the ring beyond it;
    - a second pop does the same, with a lone file where only one file is
  reached;
    - "Everything" gives today's result exactly, and a folder popped beyond the
  edge holds nothing.
  - One case for the seeds.
  - One for the link and saved setting.

  ## Forks I met
  1. **The reckoning only grows. Built so it always ends.** In one corner case
  the result runs against the rule:
     - A pop releases a folder's single file.
     - A later step brings a second file of that folder to the edge.
     - That folder becomes a membrane again.
     - A file the first one had brought in then stays, standing beside that
  membrane: two steps out.

     The alternative, recomputing from scratch each round, could flip back and
  forth without ending. I recommend the built version.
  2. **With Group Wrappers Off, "Next step" changes nothing,** and the unreached
  seeds stay too. I read your "nothing else changes there" that way. Hiding the
  seeds under Off as well would be a one-line change if you meant that.
  3. **The weights of the picture budget are unchanged.** A membrane wired only
  to released files still counts as farthest from the pin, as it does today.

  ## What I expect each picture to show (milestone 2)
  The owner's state: archetype.ts pinned, marbles on, Skin.
  - **At one hop:**
    - "Everything": about ten membranes around the hop and the unreached seeds.
    - "Next step": fewer, smaller membranes, only the folders the frontier files
  touch, and no seeds.
  - **At two and three hops:** the gap between the two grows, because
  "Everything" always holds the whole group whatever the hops, while "Next step"
  holds only the rim.
  - **In the counts:** the number of files of the scan not in the picture at all
  rises sharply under "Next step". The number of membranes and files held in
  them falls.
  - **Timing:** the partition and the packing should be faster under "Next
  step", since fewer bodies get packed.
  - **A clip of dragging Hops from 1 to 3:** the membranes break open as their
  files become hops, and new ones form one step further out. That is the "form
  and break and re-form" the owner asked about. A run of frames at each step is
  practical with the page clock; a smooth video clip is less certain.

  I'm waiting for your message that the browser is free.

  ## The compiler oracle now holds to a frozen list of known gaps: one commit,
  5205059e, on top of c9eb03ef

  **What changed.** The oracle no longer excuses anything computed at run time.
  I removed `compareFixture` and `recordedMissing` from the spec and the
  judgment.

  - **The list.** The table now carries a fixed list of known gaps, written by
  name for each map, with the edge, a reason and a date:
    - the C map: `src/processor.h uses src/processor.c`;
    - the C++ map: the same pair as `.hpp`/`.cpp`.
  - **Why the list exists.** The C-family adapters record a header's include
  where the compiler records the use of a definition. `npm run oracle:compare`
  reports this as 15 missing edges on each of those two programs.
  - **How an excuse is reported.** An excused edge keeps the oracle's cell green
  and appears under the verdict's `known` as "excused by a recorded gap, not
  agreed by the compiler". It also gets its own verdict line of rank baseline.
  Each case below has a unit test with a reading it must reject:
    - an edge the compiler has, the picture lacks and the list does not name is
  broken;
    - a listed edge the picture now draws is broken as stale;
    - a listed edge the compiler no longer has is broken as stale;
    - a drawn wire the compiler denies is still broken;
    - a map with no compiler record is still not applicable.
  - **Where it is explained.** One paragraph in each of the table's head
  comment, the judgments module's head comment and the Live Docs of `table.ts`
  and `judgments.ts` says the list is a baseline, why it exists, and that it was
  frozen after your correction of 2026-11-18.

  ### The two verdict lines for C, as the JSON shows them

  ```json
  {
    "judgment": "the subject's wires are the compiler's edges",
    "rank": "oracle",
    "outcome": "kept",
    "findings": [],
    "known": [
      "excused by a recorded gap, not agreed by the compiler: src/processor.h
  uses src/processor.c: the C-family adapters record a header's include, where
  the compiler records the use of the definition in its implementation (listed
  2026-11-18)"
    ]
  }
  {
    "judgment": "the compiler's edges the docs are known to lack at this file:
  1",
    "rank": "baseline",
    "outcome": "kept",
    "findings": [],
    "known": [
      "src/processor.h uses src/processor.c: the C-family adapters record a
  header's include, where the compiler records the use of the definition in its
  implementation (listed 2026-11-18)"
    ]
  }
  ```

› Message from general-purpose
  ## The defaults round: marbles on, hidden files as cells under the name

  **What changed for a viewer.** The Explorer now opens with every file drawn as
  a marble: glass in its kind's colour, veined blue by the files that use it
  and green by the files it uses. A directory drawn only around the pinned
  picture no longer says "+23 files". It draws one small cell per hidden file
  under its name at the reading look, and beside the name at the looks farther
  out. Both are the first value of their lists in Tuning: Marbles reads On, and
  Hidden Files reads Cells under name (then Cells beside name, then Count).
  Anyone who had kept either old default now sees the new one, because saved
  tuning only holds choices that differ from the default.

  **What I saw in the pictures** (12 pictures, in `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessi
  onHistory/2026/11/2026-11-18/Screenshots/defaults-round/`, with a README):
  - **2D, this repository** (`pathfind.ts`): at the reading look `history` shows
  its 23 cells in two rows under its name, and the path keeps its room on the
  line. At the far and overview looks the cells sit beside the name; long rows
  end in a veil, the sign that more is held than fits.
  - **2D, estate**: one cell under `PaymentService` at the reading look;
  `SqlServer` has one cell at the far look.
  - **3D**: everything is marbles, pinned and with nothing pinned, on both
  samples. The key adds a row, *Offers* / *Uses*, each beside a veined ball. No
  shader errors.
  - **Count against Cells under name, measured on both pictures at all three
  looks:** every handle where Count wrote words also shows cells. At the far
  look, five more handles show cells where Count had no room for words. No
  handle that said something with Count now says nothing.
  - **Might bother the owner:**
    - A single cell under a name is a small dim dot, easy to miss.
    - On the estate's reading look the camera's first frame contains no encasing
  handle, so the cells only appear after a pan or a zoom out. That framing is
  the same with Count.
    - At the overview, `packages` and `src` draw only the veil, with no cell to
  press.
    - From far away in 3D the veins read as texture.

  **What an old link opens as.** The rule did not change: a link writes Hidden
  Files only when it is not the default, and silence means the default. Only the
  list's order changed. So a link from November 17 or 18 that carries 2D
  settings and says nothing of Hidden Files was made with Count and now opens
  with Cells under name. A link that says "cells" opens as before. One that says
  "under" is now read as silence, which gives the same picture. Marbles are not
  carried in links.

  **Browser cases changed (11), and which kind each is:**
  - *The promise is about the old choice, so the case now sets it and says so in
  a comment:*
    1. `local-map-directories.spec.ts`, "an encasing membrane counts what it
  hides…" (flow and grid, 2 cases): chooses Count. It reads the "+N files, 2
  directories" words and the rule that a count is drawn whole or not at all.
    2. `closed-directory-looks.spec.ts:175`, "…a cell takes its file's kind's
  colour": reads the plain kind colours with marbles off. A marble's colour is
  its kind's glass tinted by its own veins.
    3. `force-graph-contained.spec.ts`, both variants (2 cases): marbles off.
  The independent reference draws a plain sphere.
    4. `history-emphasis.spec.ts`: every case runs with marbles off. The cases
  read "base colour" as one colour shared by every file of a kind (an unchanged
  `util.ts` stands for `main.ts`'s own colour); marbles make that colour differ
  from file to file. 7 cases had gone red for this.
  - *The promise holds either way, so the case runs on the new default and reads
  what it now draws:*
    5. `tuning.spec.ts`, the marbles case: opens on On, turns them off, then on
  again; both still compile and the picture changes each time.
    6. `force-graph-wires.spec.ts:338`: "no direction row" now holds as: Offers
  and Uses appear exactly twice, each beside a veined ball and never beside a
  wire.
    7. `spatial-legend.spec.ts:70`: the rows now include the veins row. For the
  check that a kind's sample matches a sphere's drawn colour, the case turns
  marbles off through Tuning first, since a marble's colour is not its kind's
  sample.
    8. `spatial-legend.spec.ts:114`: the 3D rows now include "OffersUses", with
  the wrappers on and off.
    9. `spatial-legend.spec.ts:231`: the 3D key has 7 rows; the audit's minimum
  of words goes from 6 to 8, which makes it stricter, not weaker.

  **No promise broke** under the new defaults. One gap: the marble's own shading
  has no independent reference, unlike plain spheres, which
  `force-graph-contained` checks against one.

  **Numbers:**
  - Unit tests: 2,165 of 2,165 pass.
  - Browser: 249 cases across the files that read 2D directories, 3D colours,
  the key, Tuning and links. The first run had 20 red, 11 of them from the
  defaults. The other 9 `history-emphasis` cases failed only because the history
  sample was not built in my worktree. I built it, and on the rerun all of

  - Scenario suite: 43 passed, 7 skipped as "not applicable" by its own token

  - `live-docs:generate`, `live-docs:lint` and the markdown link check are
  clean. ESLint and the tests' type-check are clean on the changed files.

  **Forks I decided:**
  - Each default is now the first value of its list (`HIDDEN_FILES_LOOKS =
  ["under", "cells", "count"]`, `MARBLE_CHOICES = ["on", "off"]`), and
  `getDefaultTuning` reads `[0]`. So the default, how a link reads silence and
  the control's fallback stay one value. The controls list their choices in that
  order too.
  - The contact sheet's `--marbles` now takes `on` or `off`. It was a flag that
  turned marbles on, which became a no-op, so there would have been no way to
  draw a sheet of plain spheres. This script was outside my named files; it is a
  small change.
  - The design document had no passage on marbles. I added one beside the 3D
  sphere paragraph, and rewrote the Hidden Files passage to state the new

  pass)

  **What changed.** The browser cases for the history's highlighting of changed
  files now run on the default look, with marbles on. Before, each case judged a

  ## The history's changed-file highlighting now runs on marbles (follow-up
  pass)

  **What changed.** The browser cases for the history's highlighting of changed
  files now run on the default look, with marbles on. Before, each case judged a
  file's "base colour" by looking at another file of the same kind. Now each
  file is compared with its own colour at the step shown: a second visitor opens
  the same address with Changed Files at None and reads the colours there. The
  cases also check whether a ball carries a marble, which the page exposes per
  sphere and per dot in its draw buffers, so no hook was needed. Under Glow, the
  changed file turns white and loses its marble, and the next step gives it
  back. Under Dim, every file keeps its marble and its own colour. Both hold for
  top-level spheres and for the dots inside the wrappers' skins. No promise in
  the file is about plain spheres, so no case turns marbles off. No comparison
  was loosened. Two titles changed wording: the wrapper case now says the rest
  "keep their own colour" where it said "their kind's colour".

  **Numbers.**
  - Browser, `history-emphasis.spec.ts` alone at one worker:
    - Before this pass, on marbles with the old stand-in: 7 of 14 red.
    - With marbles off, as I first handed it back: 14 of 14 green.
    - Now, on marbles: 14 of 14 green, in 2.7 minutes.
  - Unit, `forceGraphEmphasis.test.ts`: 13 of 13 pass. One case is new: under
  Glow and Both, `emphasizedUnits` drops the marble of the dot it whitens and
  keeps it on the rest; under Dim and None, every dot keeps its marble. No unit
  case had named that rule before.
  - Live Docs regenerated, lint clean, link check clean, tests' type-check and
  ESLint clean.

  **What I saw in the two pictures** (history sample at "Retire the legacy
  helper", step 4, which changed `main.ts`):
  - **Dim, the default** (`history-retire-legacy-helper-3d-dim.png`): `main.ts`
  stands out at once. It is the only bright marble, while every other file is a
  dark marble that keeps its bands but almost vanishes into the background. It
  reads as "this one is lit", as plain spheres did.
  - **Glow** (`history-retire-legacy-helper-3d-glow.png`): `main.ts` is a flat
  light-grey ball among glossy, full-strength striped marbles. It stands out,
  but it reads as a different kind of thing ("unpainted") more than as lit:
    - it is the only ball without bands;
    - its white looks grey next to the glossy marbles;
    - the key's only white-grey mark is the Symbols sample, and the key has no
  row for a changed file.

    This is worth the owner's eye. Dim, the default, does not have this problem.

❯ If you're finding yourself in a situation where you have time to kill, you,
  the Root Agent, are free to improve the AGENTS.md and memory documents, and
  even the root README.md for the software iteself. The way to improve them
  would involve compressing them in a way which reduces their context occupancy
  while losing as little information as possible (or even _gaining_ important
  information!). A naive approach which produces modest context size reduction
  with almost total losslessness is one which strips out the quick
  stateful-like one-liners which reaffirm statements, coupled with a date
  ("Confirmed xx/xx/xxxx as yz") which midsize models love to attach to
  documents so much. But an approach which is more holistic, more suited to an
  actual frontier model, is one which takes the information and rearranges it
  into new cleaner arrangements which result in the same understanding or
  better, but wildly more efficiently, and in ordering and grouping which meets
  the audience where it's at.

  What goes in, and what does not:

  - AGENTS.md: standing rules an agent would not follow unprompted, and pointers
    to the rest. Not the state of the work, not where a rule came from, not
    anything a current model does anyway.
  - owner.md: your durable preferences and taste, each once, with your decisive
    words and one link. Not product decisions, and not the long account of the
    episode behind each preference.
  - direction.md: per subject, the decision in force in your words, what was
    tried and rejected and why, what is open with my recommendation, and the
    ranked next work. Not how each session went, and not the same subject told
    in four lists.
  - ideas.md: the idea, whose it was, your words, and why it is not scheduled.
    Not anything decided (that is direction's) or landed (deleted, by the file's
    own rule).
  - narrative.md: turning points, what was abandoned and why, the lessons that
    became rules. Not current state, and not a session-by-session account, which
    the summaries already hold.
  - README.md: what it is, how to map your own repository, what you can ask, its
    limits. Nothing about this workspace's process.

  Where the weight is:

  - direction.md: 267 KB is the dated log and 108 KB is "Answers at forks". The
    same subject appears under Next, Open, Decisions and again by date.
    Regrouping by subject, each stance kept once with its words and link, is the
    large gain.
  - narrative.md: October and November 2026 take 198 KB in two-day chapters; the
    eleven months before take 120 KB. Those chapters are at a summary's grain.
    I would retell them at the grain of the earlier months; say so if you want
    them left.
  - AGENTS.md and README.md are small and already tuned, so they come last and
    get a light pass.

  How I will keep it lossless:

  - I read each file whole before writing; the November 12 cut was made without
    reading past line 84.
  - A script checks both ways: every quotation in the new text is word for word
    in the old, and every quotation and dated stance in the old is either in the
    new or listed with where it now lives.
  - A fresh reader compares old against new for anything a root could learn from
    one and not the other.
  - You get, per file, the sizes before and after and the list of what moved or
    was dropped.

  Order: direction, owner, narrative, ideas, then the two small ones, one commit
  each, in idle time only. The gate still runs (due about 23:17 UTC); its
  result and your links come as soon as it ends.

  ## Handback: the interaction and accessibility half of the design system

  ### Files
  1. **Committed:**
  `.mdmd/layer-2/design-system/interaction-and-accessibility.mdmd.md`, commit
  `5eef46ff` in the worktree, on top of `4856acf3`. It opens by saying it is
  current and authored, what it is for, and that `visual-foundations.mdmd.md`
  beside it is the other half. Sections:
     - 1 Principles: ten, each with its reason.
     - 2 The interaction grammar: a table of each act on each surface, the
  rules, and where one act means two things.
     - 3 States: the one look of each state, and a matrix of which states each
  component has.
     - 4 The keyboard: tab order, shortcuts, the order Escape closes things in,
  and where focus goes.
     - 5 Assistive technology: landmarks, names, roles, toggles, live regions,
  and `aria-busy` beside `data-building`.
     - 6 Feedback and orientation.
     - 7 The WCAG 2.2 A and AA table.
     - 8 Targets.
     - 9 Responsiveness.
     - 10 Portability: the content security policy, browsers, fonts, browser
  storage, what a link carries, exports.
     - 11 Words on screen.
     - 12 Glossary.

     Each rule has its term defined at first use, the rule itself, why, the file
  and line, what checks it, and a status.
  2. **Not written: `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-18/P
  robes/design-system/findings-interaction.md`.** The harness refused my Write:

  work around it. The standard already links to that path, so the root needs to
  create the file. Everything that belongs in it is below: what was read, the
